Table 10
Africa’s Development Prospects, Aid, and Climate Change
1. Q: What central question does Chapter Ten, “The two great pessimisms of today: Africa and climate after 2010,” address?
A: Show answer
The chapter asks whether two major sources of pessimism can invalidate the book’s broader confidence in human progress: persistent poverty in Africa and the possibility that climate change will undermine development. The author argues that Africa can become more prosperous and that economic progress need not lead inevitably to catastrophic climate outcomes. He connects the issues through energy use, population, institutions, aid, trade, and technology. This is the author’s thesis, not a neutral statement of present scientific consensus. A careful reader should separate the chapter’s economic arguments from its dated climate assessments and test both against newer evidence.
Study instruction:
- Summarise the author’s thesis in two sentences, then identify which parts are economic arguments, historical observations, forecasts, and scientific claims.
Key point:
- The chapter links African development with the global climate debate.
- Its central conclusion must be evaluated against evidence published since the chapter was written.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
2. Q: Why does the author describe Africa and climate as two connected “pessimisms”?
A: Show answer
The author sees the two concerns as connected because many climate scenarios assume substantial future economic growth in poorer countries, which could increase energy demand and emissions if that growth relies heavily on fossil fuels. The chapter presents a perceived dilemma: either African countries remain poor or they develop through higher-carbon energy use and face climate damage. The author rejects that binary choice. The useful analytical question is whether development can be achieved with lower emissions, greater resilience, and fair access to modern energy. Current evidence shows that this requires deliberate investment and policy, not optimism alone.
Study instruction:
- Draw a causal diagram connecting income, energy demand, energy sources, emissions, climate impacts, and adaptive capacity.
Key point:
- Development and climate are linked through energy systems and vulnerability.
- A false binary should be replaced with analysis of feasible development pathways.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
3. Q: How should readers treat the chapter’s statistics and forecasts?
A: Show answer
The chapter was written around 2010 and relies heavily on data, projections, and studies available at that time. Figures about poverty, income, population, aid, energy costs, emissions, and climate impacts are historical evidence, not current measurements. Some claims are the author’s interpretation of contested research. A sound study method records the publication date, geographic coverage, definition, baseline, and uncertainty of each statistic, then checks whether newer data confirm or revise it. Do not silently update the author’s numbers or attribute current findings to him. Present the historical argument accurately and identify later evidence separately.
Study instruction:
- Create a two-column evidence log showing each important chapter claim, its date and definition, and the newest authoritative source you can locate.
Key point:
- Historical claims must remain time-stamped.
- Later evidence should update the analysis without rewriting what the author originally argued.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
4. Q: Why is it misleading to treat Africa as one uniform economic case?
A: Show answer
Africa contains countries with different histories, institutions, climates, resource endowments, population structures, trade routes, political systems, and productive capabilities. A landlocked country faces different transport costs from a coastal trading hub; an oil exporter faces different fiscal risks from a diversified service economy; and a drought-prone rural region faces different climate pressures from a coastal city. Continental averages can conceal rapid progress in one country and severe deterioration in another. The chapter uses broad continental comparisons to frame its argument, but policy design should work at country, regional, sectoral, and household levels. Disaggregation is essential for identifying who benefits and who remains excluded.
Study instruction:
- Select three African countries with different economic structures and compare their main growth constraints, climate exposures, and infrastructure needs.
Key point:
- Continental generalisations are useful starting points, not sufficient diagnoses.
- Development policy must reflect country and community differences.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
5. Q: What does the chapter mean by Africa’s “bottom billion”?
A: Show answer
The phrase, borrowed from development writing cited by the author, refers to people left behind by growth in other regions and concentrated in severe poverty. The chapter uses an estimate that more than 600 million of this group lived in Africa at the time. That figure must not be repeated as a current count: poverty lines, purchasing-power adjustments, population estimates, and the distribution of poverty have changed. The enduring analytical point is that regional growth can coexist with large populations experiencing deprivation. Averages should therefore be supplemented with poverty depth, distribution, access to services, and progress among the poorest groups.
Study instruction:
- Find a current poverty dataset and record its year, poverty threshold, regional coverage, and treatment of purchasing power before comparing it with the chapter’s estimate.
Key point:
- The “bottom billion” is a historical framing, not a current statistical category.
- Evaluate development by whether the poorest groups share in progress.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
6. Q: Why does economic growth not automatically eliminate poverty?
A: Show answer
Growth raises the total value of production, but poverty reduction depends on who receives the additional income, what kinds of jobs are created, and whether households can access education, health care, water, electricity, transport, and markets. Growth concentrated in capital-intensive extractive industries may raise national output without generating many jobs. Inflation, debt service, conflict, weak public services, and unequal access to assets can also reduce the gains reaching poor households. The World Bank’s October 2026 update notes that projected regional growth remains insufficient by itself to substantially reduce extreme poverty or create enough jobs. Growth is necessary in many settings, but its composition and distribution matter.
Study instruction:
- Compare GDP growth with changes in real income per person, poverty, employment, wages, and access to essential services in one country.
Key point:
- Aggregate growth is not the same as inclusive development.
- Jobs, distribution, public services, and price stability determine how growth reaches households.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
7. Q: Does rapid population growth inevitably cause poverty and environmental decline?
A: Show answer
No. Population growth increases demand for food, housing, education, health care, jobs, water, and energy, but its effects depend on productivity, institutions, investment, technology, trade, and the age structure of the population. A larger population can expand markets and the potential workforce, yet it can also strain public services where investment and job creation lag. The chapter argues against treating population growth as destiny. Contemporary population projections should be read as conditional estimates rather than fixed outcomes. The United Nations’ World Population Prospects 2024 provides country and regional projections through 2100 and shows why fertility, mortality, and migration assumptions must be examined explicitly.
Study instruction:
- Use the UN population database to compare high, medium, and low projection assumptions for a country, then identify the public services most affected by each scenario.
Key point:
- Population size is a pressure or opportunity depending on economic and institutional capacity.
- Demographic projections are scenarios with uncertainty, not inevitable outcomes.
Source links:
-
- United Nations: World Population Prospects 2024
8. Q: What is the demographic transition, and why does it matter for African development?
A: Show answer
The demographic transition is the long-term shift from high birth and death rates toward lower mortality and fertility, usually accompanied by changes in life expectancy, household size, and age structure. When mortality falls before fertility declines, population growth can accelerate for a period. Later, smaller birth cohorts can reduce the share of dependent children and increase the working-age share. The timing differs greatly among countries and within them. The transition is shaped by child survival, girls’ education, access to reproductive health services, urbanisation, income, and social norms. It matters because the same population growth rate can have very different implications depending on the population’s age profile and opportunities.
Study instruction:
- Plot fertility, mortality, life expectancy, and age-dependency trends for one country and explain the stage of transition without assuming that every country follows an identical path.
Key point:
- Demographic transition changes both the pace and composition of population growth.
- Education, health, and opportunity influence how demographic change affects development.
Source links:
-
- United Nations: World Population Prospects 2024
9. Q: Under what conditions can a young population produce a demographic dividend?
A: Show answer
A demographic dividend is a potential economic gain when the working-age share of the population rises relative to dependants. It is not automatic. Young people need quality education, relevant skills, health, access to finance, reliable infrastructure, and an economy capable of creating productive work. Women must be able to participate in education and employment, and institutions must support investment and enterprise. If labour-force growth exceeds job creation, the same demographic expansion can increase underemployment, informality, migration pressure, and fiscal demands. The chapter’s optimism about population change is therefore best understood as conditional on complementary policies and productive opportunities.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a checklist of the labour-market, education, health, gender, and business conditions required to convert a rising working-age population into higher productivity.
Key point:
- A demographic dividend is a policy-dependent opportunity, not a guaranteed result.
- Job creation and human-capital investment must keep pace with labour-force growth.
Source links:
-
- United Nations: World Population Prospects 2024
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
10. Q: How do disease and poor health affect economic development?
A: Show answer
Disease reduces development through several connected channels. Illness and premature death reduce labour supply and productivity; repeated illness can interrupt schooling; households may sell productive assets or incur debt to pay for treatment; and public health systems must divert scarce resources toward preventable conditions. Malaria, HIV, tuberculosis, maternal illness, and neglected tropical diseases have different transmission patterns and policy requirements, so they should not be treated as one problem. Prevention, diagnosis, treatment, surveillance, sanitation, nutrition, and resilient health systems complement one another. The chapter is right to treat health as an economic foundation, but the size and distribution of each burden must be established with current country-specific evidence.
Study instruction:
- Map one disease pathway from prevention through diagnosis and treatment, identifying its effects on households, schools, employers, and public budgets.
Key point:
- Health is both a human-development outcome and a productive capability.
- Effective disease control requires sustained systems, not isolated distributions of supplies.
Source links:
-
- WHO: Long-Lasting Insecticidal Nets
11. Q: How do geography and landlocked status influence African trade and growth?
A: Show answer
Landlocked countries can face higher transport costs, longer delivery times, dependence on neighbouring transit states, and greater exposure to border delays or political disruption. These disadvantages can reduce the competitiveness of exports and raise the cost of imported machinery, fuel, medicines, and food. Geography is not destiny: efficient corridors, predictable customs, regional agreements, rail and road maintenance, dry ports, digital documentation, and reliable border institutions can reduce the penalty. Coastal countries also face constraints such as congestion, port inefficiency, and weak hinterland links. The correct analysis follows the entire logistics chain rather than treating access to a coastline as a sufficient measure of market access.
Study instruction:
- Trace one export route from a landlocked production area to an international port and identify time, cost, border, and infrastructure bottlenecks.
Key point:
- Geography affects trade costs but institutions and regional cooperation can alter the outcome.
- Connectivity depends on the performance of the whole transport corridor.
12. Q: Why must infrastructure investment be combined with capable institutions?
A: Show answer
Roads, ports, power systems, water networks, and telecommunications create productive opportunities only when they are planned, maintained, regulated, and connected to users. A new road can fail to support trade if border procedures remain slow; a power plant can underperform if transmission networks or utility finances are weak; and a water project can create conflict if allocation rules are unclear. Institutions determine procurement integrity, land access, service pricing, maintenance budgets, safety standards, and accountability. The chapter highlights infrastructure gaps as a constraint on African growth. A stronger interpretation is that infrastructure and institutions are complements: investment creates assets, while governance determines whether those assets deliver reliable public and economic value.
Study instruction:
- For one proposed infrastructure project, identify the capital cost, maintenance plan, operating institution, revenue or subsidy model, beneficiaries, and oversight arrangements.
Key point:
- Building an asset is not the same as delivering a durable service.
- Maintenance, regulation, financing, and accountability are part of infrastructure policy.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
13. Q: What is the resource curse, and how can mineral wealth weaken development?
A: Show answer
The resource curse describes circumstances in which abundant oil, gas, or mineral revenues coexist with weak diversification or poor development outcomes. Volatile commodity prices can make public budgets unstable; concentrated rents can encourage patronage and corruption; large foreign-exchange inflows can weaken other tradable sectors; and governments may borrow against future revenues. Resource wealth can also intensify conflict when control over extraction becomes politically valuable. These outcomes are not inevitable. Transparent contracts, competitive procurement, credible fiscal rules, public investment appraisal, independent oversight, local value addition where viable, and savings for future generations can improve the use of resource income. The chapter’s warning is most useful when treated as a governance risk rather than a universal law.
Study instruction:
- Build a risk register for a mineral-dependent budget covering price volatility, revenue forecasting, procurement, debt, environmental liabilities, and diversification.
Key point:
- Natural resources create revenue potential as well as governance and volatility risks.
- Institutions determine whether resource rents support broad-based capabilities or narrow interests.
Source links:
-
- IMF: Addressing Growth Bottlenecks in Botswana
14. Q: What does Botswana illustrate about institutions and economic development?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents Botswana as a counterexample to predictions that a landlocked, drought-prone, resource-rich country with a small population and limited infrastructure must fail. It highlights relatively secure property rights, political continuity, and institutions that pre-dated independence. The example shows that initial disadvantages do not determine a country’s future. It should not be reduced to one explanation, however. Botswana’s growth also depended on diamond revenues, macroeconomic management, political choices, and external conditions. An IMF analysis published in December 2025 notes strong post-independence growth supported by prudent macroeconomic management and diamonds, while identifying later growth, unemployment, and diversification challenges. A successful historical case is evidence of possibility, not a universal blueprint.
Study instruction:
- Separate Botswana’s initial conditions, institutional features, resource revenues, policy decisions, and later vulnerabilities before drawing lessons for another country.
Key point:
- Botswana demonstrates that adverse starting conditions do not make success impossible.
- Its experience also shows the risks of resource dependence and the need for continued diversification.
Source links:
-
- IMF: Addressing Growth Bottlenecks in Botswana
15. Q: What are secure property rights, and why do they matter for investment?
A: Show answer
Secure property rights give people and organisations reasonably predictable authority to use, transfer, inherit, lease, or benefit from assets under recognised rules. They can encourage investment because owners are less exposed to arbitrary seizure and can plan over longer periods. Secure rights also support access to credit where assets can lawfully serve as collateral. However, formal title alone is not enough: courts must be accessible, records reliable, disputes resolved fairly, and customary or communal tenure respected. Poorly designed titling can dispossess vulnerable groups or concentrate land. The chapter’s emphasis on property rights should therefore be understood as a wider requirement for legitimate, inclusive, and enforceable rules, not simply individual ownership.
Study instruction:
- Analyse a land or business-property dispute and identify the legal record, customary claims, dispute mechanism, enforcement capacity, and risks to vulnerable claimants.
Key point:
- Predictable rights can support investment and exchange.
- Legitimacy, inclusion, and accessible dispute resolution matter as much as formal registration.
Source links:
-
- IMF: Addressing Growth Bottlenecks in Botswana
16. Q: Why does the author argue that institutions often need to evolve locally rather than be imposed from above?
A: Show answer
The author’s argument is that rules work more reliably when they fit local incentives, knowledge, social relationships, and political realities. A law or market design imported without local legitimacy may exist on paper while people continue to rely on informal arrangements or powerful intermediaries. Institutions evolve through repeated interaction, bargaining, enforcement, and adaptation. This does not mean that all local practices are fair or that governments should avoid reform. It means reformers should understand existing systems, include affected groups, test implementation, and protect fundamental rights. Durable reform combines local knowledge with transparent legal standards and public accountability.
Study instruction:
- Choose one proposed institutional reform and identify whose behaviour it changes, what local practices it interacts with, and how legitimacy and enforcement will be secured.
Key point:
- Institutional reform is more than copying a statute or organisational chart.
- Local knowledge and legitimacy should inform reform while rights and accountability remain protected.
Source links:
-
- IMF: Addressing Growth Bottlenecks in Botswana
17. Q: How should colonial history be considered without treating it as destiny?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses how colonial rule, slave trading, imposed borders, extractive institutions, and post-independence policy choices may shape contemporary development. Historical legacies can affect land tenure, administrative capacity, trust, infrastructure location, political competition, and access to markets. But history does not mechanically determine current outcomes. Countries respond differently, institutions change, and present decisions can reinforce or weaken inherited constraints. A careful explanation identifies specific mechanisms rather than using colonialism as a catch-all cause or dismissing it as irrelevant. Historical analysis should be combined with evidence about current institutions, policy choices, regional conditions, and the agency of African societies themselves.
Study instruction:
- Select one historical legacy and trace a plausible mechanism linking it to a present institution or economic outcome, while identifying counterexamples and later policy changes.
Key point:
- Historical legacies shape incentives and constraints but do not remove present-day agency.
- Causal claims require specific mechanisms and evidence, not slogans.
18. Q: How can exchange and specialisation increase prosperity?
A: Show answer
Exchange allows people and firms to focus on activities in which they have relative advantages, acquire goods they do not produce efficiently, and reach markets larger than their immediate communities. Specialisation can support learning, economies of scale, innovation, and more complex production networks. But these gains depend on reliable contracts, transport, finance, information, standards, and access to markets. Trade can also create adjustment costs for workers and regions exposed to competition, so skills, mobility, social protection, and fair competition matter. The chapter’s confidence in voluntary exchange is strongest when markets are open and contestable, not when monopolies, coercion, or unequal bargaining power prevent people from sharing the gains.
Study instruction:
- Map a value chain for one African product and identify where specialisation, logistics, standards, finance, or market access limits value creation.
Key point:
- Exchange can raise productivity by widening specialisation and market access.
- Institutions and adjustment policies influence who benefits from trade.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
19. Q: Why can entrepreneurship flourish while the wider economy remains unproductive?
A: Show answer
Visible small businesses and informal trading demonstrate initiative and the ability to identify unmet needs, but they do not prove that firms can grow, invest, or create high-productivity jobs. Entrepreneurs may face expensive credit, unreliable electricity, poor transport, uncertain regulation, weak contract enforcement, limited digital connectivity, and small local markets. Many remain informal because formalisation is costly or because the benefits are unclear. Policy should reduce barriers to entry and growth while protecting workers, consumers, and the environment. The goal is not to romanticise informality or suppress it, but to create conditions in which productive firms can scale and compete fairly.
Study instruction:
- Interview or research a small enterprise and classify its constraints as finance, infrastructure, skills, market access, regulation, or institutional risk.
Key point:
- Entrepreneurial energy is valuable but needs an enabling business environment.
- Productivity and job quality matter, not only the number of small enterprises.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
20. Q: What is the author’s main criticism of foreign aid as a driver of economic growth?
A: Show answer
The author argues that aid can deliver immediate goods and services but cannot reliably create sustained economic growth by itself. He points to government-to-government transfers that may be diverted, poorly evaluated projects, tied procurement, capital-intensive schemes with weak operating capacity, and policy conditions imposed without local knowledge. He also questions whether large aid flows can weaken accountability or crowd out local enterprise. These are important risks, but the chapter’s broad scepticism should not be mistaken for proof that aid never works. Aid includes emergency relief, health interventions, infrastructure finance, technical support, and institutional partnerships, each with different objectives and evidence requirements.
Study instruction:
- Sort aid examples into humanitarian relief, service delivery, infrastructure, institutional support, and growth-oriented investment, then specify the intended result for each.
Key point:
- Aid is not one intervention and should not be judged by one outcome.
- Poor design can create dependency or distortion, but well-designed support can meet important needs.
Source links:
-
- OECD: Development Co-operation Evaluation and Effectiveness
21. Q: Does evidence support the claim that foreign aid never contributes to development?
A: Show answer
No universal conclusion is justified. The chapter cites studies and critics arguing that aggregate aid has not reliably generated sustained growth in particular periods or settings. Such findings do not establish that every aid programme fails, nor that every transfer is effective. A vaccination campaign, emergency food response, malaria-prevention programme, road investment, and budget-support operation have different causal pathways and time horizons. Some produce measurable health or welfare benefits without immediately increasing GDP; others may fail because of poor targeting, weak implementation, or political capture. The appropriate question is what intervention works, for whom, under which conditions, at what cost, and with what lasting effect.
Study instruction:
- For one aid programme, distinguish its stated objective from its outputs, outcomes, long-term impacts, and possible unintended effects.
Key point:
- Aid effectiveness is context-specific and intervention-specific.
- Evidence must be judged against the programme’s actual objective and counterfactual.
Source links:
-
- OECD: Development Co-operation Evaluation and Effectiveness
22. Q: How can the effectiveness of an aid programme be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Evaluation begins with a clear theory of change: what problem is being addressed, which activities are expected to change behaviour or conditions, and how those changes produce the intended result. Monitoring should record inputs, reach, quality, timeliness, and outputs. Evaluation then tests outcomes and longer-term effects against a credible comparison or counterfactual where feasible. Analysts should examine costs, distributional effects, implementation fidelity, unintended consequences, sustainability, and local ownership. Administrative data should be triangulated with independent surveys, qualitative evidence, and beneficiary feedback. A programme can meet its spending target yet fail to improve lives; it can also produce valuable outcomes that a narrow GDP measure misses.
Study instruction:
- Design a one-page evaluation framework containing the problem, theory of change, indicators, baseline, comparison method, cost measure, risks, and learning questions.
Key point:
- Spending and activity counts are not proof of impact.
- Credible evaluation asks what changed, for whom, at what cost, and why.
Source links:
-
- OECD: Development Co-operation Evaluation and Effectiveness
23. Q: What does the chapter’s mosquito-net example teach about aid design, and what should be updated?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a subsidised-sale example to argue that small payments may create local ownership and protect local merchants from free distributions that undermine their business. The broader lesson is to examine incentives, supply chains, user behaviour, and market effects rather than assuming that the delivery method is neutral. However, the example should not be converted into a universal rule that poor households should pay for prevention. WHO currently recommends universal coverage for people at risk of malaria through a combination of mass free distributions and continuous distribution channels, because cost must not become a barrier and coverage declines as nets wear out or populations change. Programme design must follow current health guidance and local evidence.
Study instruction:
- Compare the chapter’s historical case with current WHO guidance and identify the intended coverage, equity, continuity, and market objectives of each delivery model.
Key point:
- Incentives and local markets matter, but affordability and equitable access remain central.
- Current WHO guidance supports free mass campaigns combined with continuous distribution channels.
Source links:
-
- WHO: Long-Lasting Insecticidal Nets
24. Q: What does local ownership mean in effective development co-operation?
A: Show answer
Local ownership means that partner-country institutions and affected communities help define priorities, shape programme design, implement activities, and assess results. It is not simply government endorsement after a donor has already selected the project. Effective partnership requires alignment with country systems where appropriate, realistic capacity development, transparent financing, inclusive participation, and mutual accountability. Local ownership also requires safeguards against elite capture: national leadership does not automatically represent women, remote communities, minorities, or people affected by a project. The OECD identifies partner-country leadership, a focus on results, inclusive partnerships, transparency, and mutual accountability as core principles of effective development co-operation.
Study instruction:
- Review an aid project’s design documents and identify who selected the problem, approved the budget, chose indicators, participated in monitoring, and can challenge decisions.
Key point:
- Ownership must influence decisions, not merely appear in project language.
- Inclusion and accountability are necessary to prevent local capture.
Source links:
-
- OECD: Development Co-operation Evaluation and Effectiveness
25. Q: What safeguards can reduce corruption, waste, and dependency in aid programmes?
A: Show answer
Safeguards include transparent selection criteria, competitive procurement, published budgets and awards, segregation of duties, conflict-of-interest declarations, independent verification, accessible complaint channels, audit trails, and clear rules for asset ownership and maintenance after donor funding ends. Monitoring should compare delivery records with physical evidence and beneficiary experience. Risk-based controls are preferable to paperwork that delays essential services without reducing material risk. Donors should also examine whether their reporting demands create parallel systems that weaken local capacity. Strong safeguards protect funds while preserving operational flexibility, and they should be proportionate to the value, urgency, and risk of the intervention.
Study instruction:
- Build a control matrix for a donor-funded project showing each major risk, preventive control, detective control, responsible officer, evidence, and escalation route.
Key point:
- Accountability requires transparent decisions and verifiable delivery.
- Controls should protect beneficiaries and resources without creating unnecessary administrative burdens.
Source links:
-
- OECD: Development Co-operation Evaluation and Effectiveness
26. Q: Why can externally imposed economic reforms fail even when their stated objectives appear reasonable?
A: Show answer
Reforms can fail when they assume institutions, skills, infrastructure, market competition, or political support that do not exist. A rapid removal of controls, subsidies, or public services may impose costs on households and firms before alternatives are available. A policy can also be technically sound but politically unsustainable if affected groups have no voice or if implementation capacity is weak. The chapter criticises top-down shock therapy and argues that markets cannot simply be planned into existence. A balanced approach tests the reform’s distributional effects, sequencing, administrative requirements, and feedback mechanisms while remaining open to correcting policies that do not work.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed reform, identify prerequisites, likely winners and losers, transition support, implementation capacity, and measurable conditions for adjusting the policy.
Key point:
- Policy design must account for institutions and transition costs.
- Reform should be evidence-led, locally informed, and capable of adaptation.
Source links:
-
- OECD: Development Co-operation Evaluation and Effectiveness
27. Q: What is the chapter’s position on climate change, and how should it be assessed today?
A: Show answer
The author accepts that the atmosphere has warmed and that human activity contributes to change, but challenges the scale, timing, and economic consequences of projected warming. He argues that some scenarios rely on optimistic assumptions about future prosperity and that adaptation, innovation, and economic growth may reduce harm. This is a historical argument written using evidence available around 2010. It must be distinguished from current climate science. The IPCC’s Sixth Assessment states that human influence has unequivocally warmed the atmosphere, ocean, and land, and that climate change is already causing widespread adverse impacts. Readers should assess the author’s economic questions while not treating his older climate interpretation as the current scientific consensus.
Study instruction:
- Mark each climate claim in the chapter as an observation, model assumption, forecast, value judgement, or policy recommendation, then compare it with the IPCC assessment.
Key point:
- The author’s climate interpretation is time-bound and contested.
- Current IPCC assessments identify human-caused warming and widespread impacts as established findings.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
28. Q: What does the current scientific consensus say about human influence on climate?
A: Show answer
The IPCC concludes that it is unequivocal that human influence has warmed the atmosphere, ocean, and land. The assessment draws on multiple lines of evidence, including measured temperature changes, ocean heat, ice loss, sea-level rise, and attribution studies. This conclusion does not mean that every individual weather event is caused solely by climate change or that every regional projection is equally certain. It means that the observed long-term warming trend is primarily driven by human activities, especially greenhouse-gas emissions. Scientific confidence varies by impact and region, so responsible communication should state both the central finding and the level of confidence attached to more specific claims.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish climate trends from individual weather events and identify the evidence used in attribution science.
Key point:
- Human-caused global warming is an established conclusion of the IPCC assessment.
- Uncertainty about a particular impact does not negate the underlying warming evidence.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
29. Q: Why is climate risk better understood as a range of outcomes than as a choice between catastrophe and no problem?
A: Show answer
Climate risk depends on the amount of warming, the hazard involved, exposure, vulnerability, and the ability to adapt. Outcomes differ across regions, sectors, time horizons, and social groups. Some impacts can be managed with early warning, resilient infrastructure, improved water systems, and changes in farming; others may exceed adaptation limits as warming rises. A binary debate hides this variation and encourages poor decisions. Risk analysis should compare multiple emissions and development scenarios, identify low-probability high-consequence events, and test whether investments remain useful under different futures. The IPCC finds that risks and losses increase with warming and that limiting warming reduces damage.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a risk matrix for one African region that separates hazard, exposure, vulnerability, adaptation capacity, likelihood, and consequence.
Key point:
- Climate risk is uneven and scenario-dependent, not binary.
- Risk management must consider both likely outcomes and severe tail risks.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
30. Q: Which climate impacts are especially important for African countries?
A: Show answer
The IPCC’s Africa assessment identifies increasing heat and heatwaves, drought in some regions, heavy rainfall and flooding, water stress, sea-level rise, ecosystem disruption, and risks to agriculture, health, infrastructure, and livelihoods. Exposure is not uniform: coastal settlements, drylands, rainfed farming areas, cities with informal housing, and communities dependent on climate-sensitive work face different hazards. Some projections are highly region-specific and depend on the level of global warming. The chapter’s general discussion of drought, storms, food, and disease is relevant, but contemporary analysis must use updated regional evidence and avoid assuming that every part of Africa experiences the same climate trend.
Study instruction:
- Select one African subregion and summarise its observed climate trends, projected hazards, exposed sectors, and highest-confidence risks using the IPCC Africa chapter.
Key point:
- Climate impacts are already affecting African development sectors.
- Regional detail and confidence levels matter when choosing adaptation measures.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
31. Q: How can climate change affect African economic growth and household livelihoods?
A: Show answer
Climate impacts can reduce output through crop losses, heat-related labour productivity declines, damage to roads and buildings, disrupted tourism, water shortages, disease burdens, and interruptions to electricity or transport. The IPCC reports that climate change has reduced economic growth across Africa and cites an estimate that African GDP per capita during 1991–2010 was on average 13.6 percent lower than it would have been without human-caused warming. That figure is a research estimate, not a direct accounting measurement or a universal country-level result. Impacts differ by exposure, sector, adaptation, and national capacity, and they can widen inequality when poorer households have fewer ways to recover.
Study instruction:
- Trace one climate hazard through sector output, household income, public revenue, prices, and poverty, distinguishing observed effects from modelled estimates.
Key point:
- Climate change can affect both national output and household welfare.
- Aggregate estimates must be interpreted with their method, period, and geographic limits.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
32. Q: Why are poorer households often more vulnerable to climate hazards?
A: Show answer
Vulnerability rises when people are highly exposed but have limited assets, insurance, savings, political influence, mobility, or access to public services. Rural households may depend on rainfed agriculture and have few alternatives when crops fail. In cities, informal settlements can be exposed to floods and extreme heat while lacking drainage, secure housing, water, or health services. Women, children, older people, and people with disabilities can face additional barriers depending on their responsibilities and access to resources. The IPCC describes African vulnerability as multi-dimensional, shaped by socioeconomic, political, and environmental factors. Resilience therefore requires reducing underlying inequality as well as responding to hazards.
Study instruction:
- Compare climate exposure and recovery capacity for two households with different income, land tenure, gender, housing, and access to services.
Key point:
- The same hazard can cause very different losses across households.
- Social protection, services, rights, and access to resources are part of climate adaptation.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
33. Q: What practical measures can help African communities adapt to climate change?
A: Show answer
Adaptation options include early-warning systems, climate information services, drought-tolerant crops, diversified livelihoods, soil and water conservation, irrigation where sustainable, improved drainage, resilient roads, public-health preparedness, insurance, and social-protection programmes. Their effectiveness depends on local conditions, affordability, institutions, maintenance, and the ability of communities to use them. A technology should not be distributed without checking whether farmers can obtain seed, repair equipment, reach markets, or access water. The IPCC finds that many adaptation options can reduce present-day risks, but their effectiveness becomes more limited as warming increases. Adaptation should therefore be planned with mitigation and development rather than treated as a substitute for reducing emissions.
Study instruction:
- Choose one climate-sensitive livelihood and compare at least three adaptation options by cost, beneficiaries, maintenance, feasibility, and residual risk.
Key point:
- Effective adaptation is locally appropriate and supported by institutions and finance.
- Adaptation reduces risk but cannot offset every impact at higher warming levels.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
- World Bank: Scaling Innovation to Boost Food Systems and Support Job Creation
34. Q: What is maladaptation, and why can a well-intentioned project increase climate risk?
A: Show answer
Maladaptation occurs when an action intended to reduce climate risk increases vulnerability, shifts risk to another group or sector, locks in high emissions, or damages the ecosystems on which resilience depends. A large dam designed using outdated rainfall assumptions may become unreliable under changing hydrology; an afforestation project placed in a natural grassland can damage biodiversity and water systems; and flood protection can encourage new construction in an area that remains exposed to extreme events. The IPCC warns that responses in one sector can create risks in another without cross-sectoral and long-term planning. Adaptation appraisal should therefore test environmental, social, economic, and future-climate effects before implementation.
Study instruction:
- For one proposed adaptation project, identify who benefits, who may bear new risks, what ecosystems are affected, and how the project performs under alternative climate scenarios.
Key point:
- Good intentions do not guarantee climate resilience.
- Projects must be screened for cross-sector effects, lock-in, and distributional harm.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
35. Q: What is the difference between climate mitigation and climate adaptation?
A: Show answer
Mitigation reduces the causes of climate change by lowering greenhouse-gas emissions or increasing removals, for example through clean electricity, energy efficiency, low-emission transport, and protection of carbon-rich ecosystems. Adaptation reduces harm from climate effects that are occurring or expected, for example through heat planning, water management, resilient infrastructure, crop diversification, and early warning. The two are complementary. Mitigation reduces the scale of future hazards; adaptation manages risks that cannot be avoided. A development plan that funds only adaptation leaves future warming largely unchecked, while a plan that funds only mitigation can neglect communities already facing losses. Both must be integrated with poverty reduction and equity.
Study instruction:
- Classify ten climate actions as mitigation, adaptation, both, or neither, and explain the mechanism behind each classification.
Key point:
- Mitigation addresses the drivers of climate change.
- Adaptation manages the consequences, and both are needed for climate-resilient development.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
36. Q: Does climate action require African countries to choose between prosperity and modern energy access?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents a stark trade-off between fossil-fuelled growth and continued poverty, but this framing is too narrow. Modern energy access is essential for health, education, businesses, public services, and industrial development. The relevant choices include which energy sources, grids, storage, pricing systems, and financing arrangements can deliver reliable and affordable service while limiting long-term emissions and pollution. Low-carbon pathways are not costless or automatic: they require capital, technical capacity, reliable networks, and solutions for communities that cannot pay commercial prices. The policy challenge is to expand energy access and productive use while avoiding unnecessary high-carbon lock-in and ensuring that the transition is fair.
Study instruction:
- Compare two electricity expansion plans on access, reliability, affordability, emissions, financing, local employment, and long-term operating costs.
Key point:
- Energy access and climate mitigation should be planned together.
- A just transition requires finance, infrastructure, technology, and attention to affordability.
Source links:
-
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
37. Q: Why is reliable electricity access a development issue rather than merely a household convenience?
A: Show answer
Electricity supports lighting, refrigeration, communication, water pumping, health facilities, schools, manufacturing, digital services, and business continuity. Access is not the same as reliability: a household may be connected but still face frequent outages, high tariffs, or insufficient power for productive equipment. The IEA reported in November 2026 that the number of people without electricity in sub-Saharan Africa fell by a record eight million in 2025, yet population growth outpaced new access in 20 countries in the region. This shows both progress and a continuing delivery challenge. Planning should measure connections, affordability, quality, reliability, and productive use rather than counting grid connections alone.
Study instruction:
- Design an electricity-access scorecard covering connection, hours of service, outage frequency, tariff affordability, clean cooking, and productive uses.
Key point:
- Connection counts do not capture the quality or economic value of electricity service.
- Access gains must keep pace with population growth and reliability needs.
Source links:
-
- IEA: A Record Number of People in Africa Gained Electricity Access in 2025
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
38. Q: What energy-system options can expand access in remote African communities?
A: Show answer
Options include extending the national grid, building mini-grids, and installing stand-alone systems such as solar home systems. Grid extension can serve dense settlements and support larger loads, but may be costly in remote areas and depends on utility capacity. Mini-grids can serve clusters of homes, schools, clinics, and businesses, but require viable demand, maintenance, tariffs, and regulation. Stand-alone systems can reach dispersed households quickly, although smaller systems may provide only basic services. The IEA emphasises a mix of grid and decentralised solutions and identifies affordability and investment as major barriers. Technology choice should follow geospatial demand, service needs, lifecycle cost, and local operating capacity.
Study instruction:
- Compare grid extension, mini-grid, and stand-alone solar for a remote settlement using demand density, capital cost, reliability, maintenance, tariff, and productive-use criteria.
Key point:
- No single electricity technology fits every settlement.
- A least-cost access plan combines technologies with reliable financing and service standards.
Source links:
-
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
- IEA: A Record Number of People in Africa Gained Electricity Access in 2025
39. Q: How can African economies reduce emissions while expanding productive capacity?
A: Show answer
A credible pathway combines efficient energy use, renewable generation where technically and economically suitable, stronger transmission and distribution, storage and flexible demand, clean cooking, low-emission transport, resilient cities, and protection of forests and other carbon-rich ecosystems. Industrial development also needs reliable power, affordable finance, skills, standards, and access to regional markets. Countries differ in resource endowments and development priorities, so transition plans should be country-specific and transparent about costs and trade-offs. The objective is not simply to install renewable equipment; it is to provide dependable energy services and improve productivity while reducing pollution and future emissions exposure. International finance can help address high upfront costs and risk.
Study instruction:
- Select one sector such as electricity, transport, industry, or cooking and draft a transition pathway with milestones, financing, reliability targets, and safeguards for affected workers and households.
Key point:
- Decarbonisation requires a whole-system plan, not a single technology.
- Productivity, reliability, affordability, and fairness must be assessed together.
Source links:
-
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
40. Q: Why is climate finance important for African development?
A: Show answer
Many African countries contribute relatively little to historical global greenhouse-gas emissions yet face substantial climate exposure and constrained fiscal capacity. Climate finance can support adaptation, mitigation, technology transfer, resilient infrastructure, early-warning systems, and recovery from climate-related losses. Its usefulness depends on additionality, predictability, accessibility, affordability, and alignment with national and local priorities. Loans can increase debt pressure if projects do not generate sufficient economic or social returns, so grants and concessional finance may be especially important for adaptation and public goods. Finance should be tracked from commitment to actual disbursement and results, with transparent reporting and community participation.
Study instruction:
- Review a climate-finance proposal and distinguish its mitigation and adaptation components, funding instrument, debt implications, beneficiaries, and measurable outcomes.
Key point:
- Climate finance can expand resilience and lower-carbon development where domestic resources are constrained.
- The terms, accessibility, and results of finance matter as much as the headline amount.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
41. Q: How should policymakers assess the trade-offs of fossil-fuel development?
A: Show answer
Fossil-fuel projects can provide dispatchable energy, export revenue, industrial feedstocks, and public income, but they also create greenhouse-gas emissions, local air pollution, price exposure, environmental liabilities, and the risk of assets becoming uneconomic as technology and policy change. A decision should compare the project with alternatives using full lifecycle costs, reliability, financing terms, foreign-exchange exposure, employment, health impacts, emissions, and decommissioning obligations. It should also ask whether revenues will be invested productively and whether affected communities receive fair benefits. The chapter frames energy as a development dilemma; modern analysis should test each project rather than assume either that all fossil investment is necessary or that every alternative is immediately feasible.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an options appraisal for a proposed energy project that includes financial, reliability, climate, health, distributional, and stranded-asset risks.
Key point:
- Energy choices have economic, environmental, fiscal, and intergenerational consequences.
- Project decisions should be based on transparent comparison of feasible alternatives.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
- IEA: Financing Electricity Access in Africa
42. Q: What role does the discount rate play in climate-policy decisions?
A: Show answer
A discount rate converts future costs and benefits into present values. A higher rate gives less weight to distant future effects, while a lower rate gives them greater present value. Climate policy is unusually sensitive to this choice because emissions today can create damages over many decades or centuries. The rate reflects not only market returns and economic assumptions but also ethical judgements about future generations, uncertainty, and the risk of severe irreversible harm. The chapter discusses disputes over climate-economics calculations, but there is no single discount rate that removes the underlying value choices. Analysts should show sensitivity across plausible rates and explain the consequences rather than conceal assumptions in one headline figure.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate a simplified long-term climate project using several discount rates and explain how the ranking of options changes.
Key point:
- Discounting is both a technical method and an ethical choice about time.
- Sensitivity analysis is essential when impacts extend across generations.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
43. Q: Why should the chapter’s discussion of climate models and projections be treated cautiously?
A: Show answer
Climate projections are conditional estimates based on emissions pathways, socioeconomic assumptions, physical processes, and model limitations. They are not simple predictions of one exact future. The chapter challenges assumptions in older scenarios, including assumptions about future prosperity and energy use. Such scrutiny is legitimate, but it must use the full range of current evidence rather than selecting a single older study or disputed parameter. Modern assessments compare multiple scenarios, evaluate model performance, attribute observed changes, and express confidence levels. A projection should be examined for its baseline, time horizon, spatial scale, emissions pathway, uncertainty range, and treatment of adaptation. Uncertainty is a reason to manage risk, not to ignore it.
Study instruction:
- Choose one climate projection and record its scenario, baseline period, time horizon, region, confidence level, and key assumptions.
Key point:
- Projections are conditional and should be read with their assumptions and uncertainty.
- Current assessments are broader and more systematic than isolated studies cited in older debates.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
44. Q: How do ecosystems contribute to climate resilience and economic development?
A: Show answer
Ecosystems support livelihoods through soil formation, water regulation, pollination, fisheries, grazing, timber, tourism, coastal protection, and carbon storage. Degraded forests, wetlands, mangroves, reefs, and grasslands can increase exposure to floods, erosion, heat, and biodiversity loss. Nature-based approaches can complement engineered infrastructure, but they must be ecologically appropriate and respect land rights and local knowledge. For example, planting trees in the wrong ecosystem can damage native grasslands or water availability. The IPCC highlights both the value of ecosystem-based adaptation and the risk of maladaptation when interventions ignore local ecology. Conservation is therefore not separate from development; it is part of the asset base on which many communities depend.
Study instruction:
- Identify the ecosystem services used by a local community and assess how degradation or restoration would affect livelihoods, risk, and distribution of benefits.
Key point:
- Healthy ecosystems provide economic services and reduce some climate risks.
- Nature-based solutions must fit the ecosystem and respect local rights.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
45. Q: How can agricultural innovation improve food security under climate change?
A: Show answer
Useful approaches include drought- or heat-tolerant crop varieties, improved seed systems, soil-health management, water harvesting, efficient irrigation, agroforestry where appropriate, crop and livelihood diversification, pest surveillance, and timely climate information. Innovation must reach farmers through trusted extension, affordable inputs, local research, functioning markets, storage, transport, and access to finance. Technology should be assessed for yield stability, nutrition, cost, water use, biodiversity, and suitability for different farm sizes. A World Bank initiative announced in March 2026 is scaling climate-smart innovations and advisory services across several African countries. Programme results should be checked independently and not assumed to apply uniformly beyond the targeted areas.
Study instruction:
- Design a farm-level adaptation package for one agroecological zone and specify the evidence, delivery partners, financing, and indicators needed to assess adoption and food-security outcomes.
Key point:
- Agricultural innovation works best as a package of technology, information, institutions, and market access.
- Local validation and monitoring are needed before scaling a solution.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Scaling Innovation to Boost Food Systems and Support Job Creation
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
46. Q: Why are regional cooperation and cross-border institutions important for African development and climate resilience?
A: Show answer
Many development challenges cross national boundaries. Roads, ports, customs systems, electricity interconnectors, river basins, disease surveillance, migration routes, food markets, and climate risks often require coordinated action. A country may invest in a transport corridor but depend on a neighbour’s border procedures; a dam may alter water availability downstream; and a regional power pool can improve reliability only if transmission, regulation, and payment systems work across borders. Regional cooperation can enlarge markets and spread knowledge, but it requires credible agreements, shared data, dispute resolution, and fair allocation of costs and benefits. National policy remains important, yet some constraints cannot be solved by one state acting alone.
Study instruction:
- Map one cross-border challenge and identify the countries, institutions, data, financing, and dispute mechanisms needed for a coordinated response.
Key point:
- Regional integration can reduce fragmentation and improve scale.
- Cross-border projects need enforceable rules and shared accountability.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
47. Q: How can policymakers use scenario planning without pretending to predict the future?
A: Show answer
Scenario planning develops several internally consistent futures to test whether policies remain useful under different conditions. For African development, scenarios might vary population growth, productivity, commodity prices, governance, trade access, climate hazards, energy technology, finance, and conflict. The purpose is not to declare which future will occur but to reveal dependencies, vulnerabilities, and robust investments. Sensitivity analysis can show which assumptions change the result most. Policies that perform reasonably well across several plausible futures are often more resilient than plans built around one optimistic forecast. Scenario work should be transparent about uncertainty, distributional effects, and the possibility of unexpected shocks.
Study instruction:
- Build three scenarios for a country through 2040 and test how a proposed infrastructure or energy investment performs under each one.
Key point:
- Scenarios support decisions under uncertainty; they are not precise predictions.
- Robust policy is designed to adapt as evidence and conditions change.
Source links:
-
- United Nations: World Population Prospects 2024
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
48. Q: Which indicators provide a better assessment of African development than GDP growth alone?
A: Show answer
A balanced dashboard should include real GDP per capita, labour productivity, poverty incidence and depth, employment quality, wages, inflation, health, education, nutrition, access to electricity and clean cooking, water and sanitation, transport reliability, private investment, debt service, governance, and resilience to climate shocks. Indicators should be disaggregated by gender, age, location, income, and other relevant characteristics. A single national average can hide exclusion or uneven regional performance. Time series and definitions must be consistent, and leading indicators should be combined with outcome measures. The World Bank’s 2026 update illustrates why growth, jobs, inflation, debt, climate risks, and development assistance need to be considered together.
Study instruction:
- Construct a development dashboard with at least eight indicators, stating each definition, source, reporting period, disaggregation, and policy relevance.
Key point:
- Development is multidimensional and cannot be inferred from GDP alone.
- Good indicators reveal distribution, service quality, and resilience as well as output.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
49. Q: How should a policymaker apply the chapter’s development argument to a country facing poverty, weak infrastructure, and climate risk?
A: Show answer
Begin with a country-specific diagnosis rather than a continental stereotype. Identify the binding constraints on productivity and welfare, including energy, transport, health, skills, finance, land administration, market access, institutional trust, and climate exposure. Prioritise investments that produce broad benefits and can be operated and maintained. Support enterprise and trade while protecting competition and vulnerable households. Integrate adaptation into infrastructure, agriculture, health, and urban planning, and reduce emissions where feasible without denying people reliable energy. Use transparent budgets, local participation, independent evaluation, and indicators that track jobs, poverty, service quality, and resilience. The chapter’s optimism is useful when translated into testable policies and accountability.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a five-priority country strategy and justify each priority with evidence, implementation capacity, distributional analysis, financing, and measurable outcomes.
Key point:
- Development strategy should connect productivity, institutions, human capabilities, and climate resilience.
- Priorities must be country-specific, financed, measurable, and open to revision.
Source links:
-
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
- IPCC AR6 Working Group II: Africa
50. Q: What integrated lessons should readers retain from Chapter Ten?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges fatalism by showing that poverty, weak institutions, disease, infrastructure gaps, and climate pressures do not make development impossible. Its strongest enduring lessons are to examine incentives, support productive exchange, build institutions that people can trust, invest in health and infrastructure, and evaluate aid by results rather than intentions. Its climate conclusions, however, must be treated as a historical argument and updated using current scientific assessments. The IPCC documents human-caused warming and serious risks in Africa, while current economic and energy data show both progress and persistent constraints. Rational optimism is therefore best understood as confidence in human agency combined with rigorous evidence, risk awareness, and responsibility for future generations.
Study instruction:
- Write a balanced review identifying three enduring insights from the chapter, three claims that require updating, and three practical policies that connect inclusive growth with climate resilience.
Key point:
- Optimism is strongest when it is evidence-based, conditional, and open to correction.
- Sustainable prosperity requires institutions, human capabilities, energy access, and credible climate action.
Source links:
-
- IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report: Summary for Policymakers
- World Bank: Sub-Saharan Africa Growth Update, 6 October 2026
